  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, R41.840\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, nice to meet you too. Honestly, I wouldn't even be here if it wasn't for, well, basically everyone in my life getting on my case lately. \n\nWhat really brought me in is that people just keep saying I’m not listening to them. And it’s causing a real lot of friction, you know? Like, my wife will be talking straight to me, standing right there in the kitchen, looking right in my eyes. She'll be going over the schedule for the week or telling me something important, and I swear to you, I think I’m paying attention. I’m looking right at her. But then, I don't know, a minute goes by and I suddenly realize I only caught maybe half of what she said. Or less. And then I have to stop her and ask her to say it all over again, which just makes her absolutely furious because she thinks I don't care about what she's saying or that I'm ignoring her on purpose. \n\nAnd it’s not just at home, it’s at work too. I work production, over at the stamping plant, and my supervisor will come over to my station—and it's loud in there, sure, but he'll be talking right to my face about a change in the shift quota or a parts issue. I’ll be nodding along, saying 'yep, got it, boss,' and the second he walks away, I’m standing there realizing my brain just completely dropped the ball. I have to go track him down on the factory floor and ask him to repeat the whole thing. He's getting really ticked off about it, looking at me like I'm an idiot or like I'm trying to mess with him. I'm really not! It's just... the words go in and then they just vanish, and I'm tired of making everyone mad at me all the time. I can't afford to lose this job over something so stupid, so here I am.\n\nDoctor: When people are talking to you, do you usually hear the words clearly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, as far as I know, my ears work perfectly fine. It's definitely not a volume thing, if that's what you mean. Like, I hear the actual sound coming out of their mouths loud and clear. It's not like they're mumbling or talking underwater or anything like that. \n\nTake my wife, for example—she speaks perfectly clear, enunciates everything, especially when she's getting frustrated with me. I hear every single syllable. I hear the tone of her voice, I know she's speaking English, I know she's standing right there. The physical sound is definitely making it into my ears. Even at the plant, you know, with all the machines going—the stamping presses are really loud, sure, it's a lot of heavy metal-on-metal crashing all day long—but when my supervisor gets right up in my face to talk, I can hear his voice over the background noise just fine. \n\nSo it's not my hearing. I don't need a hearing aid or anything like that. It’s more like... well, it’s like the words hit my eardrum, and they’re perfectly clear words, but the wire connecting my ear to my actual brain is just... unplugged or something. The sound goes in, it bounces around in my head for a split second, and then it just evaporates into thin air before my brain can actually translate it into an actual instruction or meaning. I'll even catch myself thinking, \"Wow, she's talking right now, I should listen,\" but the actual words just slide right off me. So, yeah, the hearing part is totally fine. It’s the keeping it in my head part that's completely shot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble listening or following what people said when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh yeah, definitely. I mean, my mom used to say I was born with a motor running and no steering wheel. Even way back in elementary school, I was exactly like this. Probably worse, honestly, because when you're a kid you don't even try to hide it. \n\nI remember my teachers were constantly—and I mean constantly—moving my desk around. It was like a game of musical chairs but just for me. First grade, second grade, third grade, it didn't matter. One week I’d be in the front row so the teacher could keep an eye on me, the next week I’d be banished to the back corner because I was distracting the kids next to me, then I'd be right up against the teacher's desk. They just couldn't figure out what to do with me because I absolutely could not stay put in my seat. I was always up sharpening my pencil, or looking out the window, or just standing up for no reason. \n\nAnd the talking! Man, I could not keep my mouth shut. If the teacher asked a question, I’d just blurt out the answer. I wouldn't even think to raise my hand. The answer would just pop into my head and shoot right out of my mouth before I even realized I was talking. It drove my teachers crazy. They'd always be saying, \"Raise your hand, wait your turn,\" but I just couldn't seem to hold it in. And it wasn't just answering questions either. If the kid next to me was doing a worksheet, I'd lean over and start telling them how to do it, or I'd grab their crayon because I thought they were coloring something wrong. I was always butting into other kids' work, getting all up in their business. \n\nIt was like that all the way up until middle school, really. Just constant notes home, parent-teacher conferences where they'd tell my parents I wasn't listening, I was disruptive, I couldn't sit still. So yeah, this isn't a new thing at all. It's been happening pretty much my whole life, just... I guess the consequences are a lot bigger now than getting a frowny face sticker on a report card, you know?\n\nDoctor: Besides when people are talking to you, do you also have trouble staying focused on things like paperwork, reading, or other tasks?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. I mean, sure, nobody likes doing paperwork, right? It's boring. But if I have to sit down and read a manual for a new machine at the plant, or if my wife makes me sit down and look over the bills, I can do it. I might grumble about it, but I can get through it. It's not like my brain just shuts off the way it does when people are talking to me. \n\nLike, if I have a piece of paper in front of me, the words are just sitting there. They aren't going anywhere. If I zone out for a second, I can just look back down and pick up right where I left off. I don't have to go ask the paper to repeat itself, you know? So reading isn't really the issue. I read the sports page every morning while I have my coffee, and I don't have any trouble following the articles or keeping track of the stats. \n\nAnd as far as other tasks go, especially at work, I'm actually pretty good at staying on top of things as long as I'm moving. Like I said, I work production, so I'm on my feet all day. I'm checking the presses, I'm moving pallets, I'm clearing jams. I can stay focused on all that physical stuff for hours. It's actually when things slow down or when I have to stand in one spot and just listen to somebody that I start having problems. But if I'm actively doing something with my hands, or if I'm reading something at my own pace where I can control how fast the information is coming at me, I'm generally okay. It's just the talking. The talking is what gets me every single time.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, do other sights or sounds around you easily pull your attention away?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's not like a squirrel runs by the window and I completely lose my train of thought, you know? It's not the stuff going on around me that's the problem. Like at the plant, there's always a million things happening—forklifts beeping, guys shouting over the machines, sparks flying from the welding stations—and none of that really pulls my attention away when someone is talking to me. I can tune all that out just fine. \n\nIt's more like... the distraction comes from inside my own head, not from the outside world. If my supervisor is telling me about a change in the shift schedule, I'm not looking over his shoulder at a forklift. I'm looking right at him. But my brain just decides, right in that exact moment, that it's the perfect time to wonder if I left the garage door open, or to try and remember the name of that actor from that movie we watched last night, or to start thinking about what I'm going to make for dinner. It's like my own thoughts are just shouting over the person who's standing right in front of me. \n\nSo it's not the environment. The environment could be completely silent, like if I'm sitting in the living room with my wife and the TV is off and the dog is asleep, and she starts talking to me about her day. There are zero distractions in the room. Nothing to look at, nothing to listen to except her. But even then, my brain will just start spinning its own wheels, and before I know it, she's looking at me waiting for an answer and I have absolutely no idea what she just said for the last two minutes. So, yeah, it's not the outside sights and sounds at all. It's just my own head getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: In your day-to-day life now, do you often lose or misplace things you need, like keys, your phone, tools, or paperwork?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm actually pretty organized when it comes to my stuff. It's funny, because you'd think with how my brain bounces around, I'd be losing things left and right, but I actually have a real system down. \n\nLike with my keys and my wallet, the second I walk in the door at home, they go right into this little wooden bowl we keep on the table in the entryway. My wife bought it at a craft fair a few years ago—it's got this nice cherry finish—and I just trained myself to drop everything in there immediately. If I don't put it in the bowl, I know I'll put it down on the kitchen counter, or the coffee table, or leave it in my jacket pocket, and then I'd be running around like a madman the next morning. But because of the bowl, I always know exactly where they are. Same thing with my phone. It's either in my front right pocket, or it's on the charger by the bed. There's no in-between. \n\nAnd at work, man, you can't be losing tools on the production floor. That's a huge safety hazard, plus it just slows everything down. I've got my tool belt set up exactly the same way every single day. Wrench goes here, gauge goes there. If I take something out to adjust a machine, it goes right back into its specific pouch the second I'm done. I don't set it down on the line, I don't hand it to the guy next to me. It goes back in the belt. I've been doing this job for almost twenty years, and I can probably count on one hand the number of times I've actually misplaced a tool. \n\nEven with paperwork, like the timecards or the shift reports, I just have a clipboard. I clip it in, I fill it out, I hand it to the foreman. I don't leave it sitting around to get buried under other stuff. So yeah, losing things really isn't an issue for me at all. I've just built these habits over the years to make sure everything has a place, and I stick to it pretty religiously. If I didn't, maybe it would be a problem, but the way I do things now, I keep track of my stuff just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things in your daily life, like errands, chores, or returning calls?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty good about getting things done. It's kind of like what I was saying about my tools and my wallet—I just have my routines, you know? Like with chores around the house, my wife and I have this system we've been using since we bought the place back in '08. It's a modest house, three beds, one bath, but keeping it up takes work. So Saturday mornings, I wake up, I make the coffee—always a dark roast, none of that flavored stuff—and while it's brewing, I take the trash out. Then I mow the lawn if it's summer, or shovel the walk if it's winter. It's just muscle memory at this point. I don't even have to think about it, I just do it. \n\nAnd errands, same deal. If we need groceries or something from the hardware store, I usually just hit it on my way home from the plant. I get off at 3:30, so I beat the rush hour traffic, and I just swing by the store. My wife will text me a list, and I just go right down the aisles. I don't really forget to do it because it's just part of the drive home. If I go straight home and sit down, yeah, it's harder to get back up and go out again, but as long as I'm already in the truck and moving, I knock it out. \n\nReturning calls... I mean, I'm not much of a phone guy anyway. If someone calls me and leaves a voicemail, I'll listen to it, and usually I just call them right back. Or I'll text them. Most of the guys at work just text anyway. If it's something official, like the dentist reminding me about an appointment, I just write it on the calendar we keep on the fridge. It's this big magnetic calendar with a picture of a golden retriever on it—looks just like the dog we had when I was a kid, actually. But yeah, I write it on there, and then I don't forget. So I wouldn't say I have a problem with forgetting to do things. I just keep moving and stick to the routine, and everything pretty much gets handled.\n\nDoctor: When you listen to a podcast or the radio, do you often realize you missed what was being said and have to rewind or ask someone what you missed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's this big magnetic calendar with a picture of a golden retriever on it—looks just like the dog we had when I was a kid, actually. But yeah, I write it on there, and then I don't forget. Yeah, so like I was saying about Dave pulling me aside... to answer your question, yes. It's actually getting pretty serious. Like, really bad. Dave actually had to give me a formal warning. Two of them, actually, in the last six months. They bring you into this little glass office off the floor, and HR gets involved, and they print out this official form—it's always on this weird yellow paper, I don't know why they color-code it like that, but you have to sign it saying you understand the corrective action. And it really shook me up because, you know, Dave looked me right in the eye and said, "Look, if you miss a loose fitting and one of these industrial pumps blows a seal during pressure testing, someone could get seriously hurt." And he's right. These things operate under massive pressure. If I cause a safety problem on the line because I can't just stand still and check the boxes properly... man, I worry I could actually lose my job. I really do. And I’ve been there forever. Starting over at 42 without a college degree? That keeps me up at night. 

And then, you know, it's not just the plant. It bleeds into home, too. I know I was bragging earlier about my Saturday morning routines with the coffee and the trash, but... well, the truth is, my wife and my kids, they get incredibly frustrated with me lately. Because a lot of the time, I don't actually help things run smoothly. I end up completely disrupting the routines. Like, I'll go to take that trash out, but on the way, I'll notice the hinge on the screen door is squeaking. So I drop the trash bag on the porch, go to the garage to find the WD-40, realize my workbench is a mess, start organizing my socket wrenches, and suddenly it's two hours later. The trash has been ripped into by a raccoon on the porch, the coffee I made burned to the bottom of the pot inside, and my wife is just... she's at her wit's end. 

She tells me she just needs me to be a partner and help keep the house moving, but instead, I just throw a wrench in everything because I can't just do the one simple thing I set out to do. Even with the kids, when they're trying to get ready for school, I'm just buzzing around the kitchen, getting in their way, starting to make pancakes and then wandering off to check the weather on the TV. So, yeah... I guess when you put it all together, it really is causing serious trouble in pretty much every part of my life.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, R41.840\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, nice to meet you too. Honestly, I wouldn't even be here if it wasn't for, well, basically everyone in my life getting on my case lately. \n\nWhat really brought me in is that people just keep saying I’m not listening to them. And it’s causing a real lot of friction, you know? Like, my wife will be talking straight to me, standing right there in the kitchen, looking right in my eyes. She'll be going over the schedule for the week or telling me something important, and I swear to you, I think I’m paying attention. I’m looking right at her. But then, I don't know, a minute goes by and I suddenly realize I only caught maybe half of what she said. Or less. And then I have to stop her and ask her to say it all over again, which just makes her absolutely furious because she thinks I don't care about what she's saying or that I'm ignoring her on purpose. \n\nAnd it’s not just at home, it’s at work too. I work production, over at the stamping plant, and my supervisor will come over to my station—and it's loud in there, sure, but he'll be talking right to my face about a change in the shift quota or a parts issue. I’ll be nodding along, saying 'yep, got it, boss,' and the second he walks away, I’m standing there realizing my brain just completely dropped the ball. I have to go track him down on the factory floor and ask him to repeat the whole thing. He's getting really ticked off about it, looking at me like I'm an idiot or like I'm trying to mess with him. I'm really not! It's just... the words go in and then they just vanish, and I'm tired of making everyone mad at me all the time. I can't afford to lose this job over something so stupid, so here I am.\n\nDoctor: When people are talking to you, do you usually hear the words clearly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, as far as I know, my ears work perfectly fine. It's definitely not a volume thing, if that's what you mean. Like, I hear the actual sound coming out of their mouths loud and clear. It's not like they're mumbling or talking underwater or anything like that. \n\nTake my wife, for example—she speaks perfectly clear, enunciates everything, especially when she's getting frustrated with me. I hear every single syllable. I hear the tone of her voice, I know she's speaking English, I know she's standing right there. The physical sound is definitely making it into my ears. Even at the plant, you know, with all the machines going—the stamping presses are really loud, sure, it's a lot of heavy metal-on-metal crashing all day long—but when my supervisor gets right up in my face to talk, I can hear his voice over the background noise just fine. \n\nSo it's not my hearing. I don't need a hearing aid or anything like that. It’s more like... well, it’s like the words hit my eardrum, and they’re perfectly clear words, but the wire connecting my ear to my actual brain is just... unplugged or something. The sound goes in, it bounces around in my head for a split second, and then it just evaporates into thin air before my brain can actually translate it into an actual instruction or meaning. I'll even catch myself thinking, \"Wow, she's talking right now, I should listen,\" but the actual words just slide right off me. So, yeah, the hearing part is totally fine. It’s the keeping it in my head part that's completely shot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble listening or following what people said when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh yeah, definitely. I mean, my mom used to say I was born with a motor running and no steering wheel. Even way back in elementary school, I was exactly like this. Probably worse, honestly, because when you're a kid you don't even try to hide it. \n\nI remember my teachers were constantly—and I mean constantly—moving my desk around. It was like a game of musical chairs but just for me. First grade, second grade, third grade, it didn't matter. One week I’d be in the front row so the teacher could keep an eye on me, the next week I’d be banished to the back corner because I was distracting the kids next to me, then I'd be right up against the teacher's desk. They just couldn't figure out what to do with me because I absolutely could not stay put in my seat. I was always up sharpening my pencil, or looking out the window, or just standing up for no reason. \n\nAnd the talking! Man, I could not keep my mouth shut. If the teacher asked a question, I’d just blurt out the answer. I wouldn't even think to raise my hand. The answer would just pop into my head and shoot right out of my mouth before I even realized I was talking. It drove my teachers crazy. They'd always be saying, \"Raise your hand, wait your turn,\" but I just couldn't seem to hold it in. And it wasn't just answering questions either. If the kid next to me was doing a worksheet, I'd lean over and start telling them how to do it, or I'd grab their crayon because I thought they were coloring something wrong. I was always butting into other kids' work, getting all up in their business. \n\nIt was like that all the way up until middle school, really. Just constant notes home, parent-teacher conferences where they'd tell my parents I wasn't listening, I was disruptive, I couldn't sit still. So yeah, this isn't a new thing at all. It's been happening pretty much my whole life, just... I guess the consequences are a lot bigger now than getting a frowny face sticker on a report card, you know?\n\nDoctor: Besides when people are talking to you, do you also have trouble staying focused on things like paperwork, reading, or other tasks?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. I mean, sure, nobody likes doing paperwork, right? It's boring. But if I have to sit down and read a manual for a new machine at the plant, or if my wife makes me sit down and look over the bills, I can do it. I might grumble about it, but I can get through it. It's not like my brain just shuts off the way it does when people are talking to me. \n\nLike, if I have a piece of paper in front of me, the words are just sitting there. They aren't going anywhere. If I zone out for a second, I can just look back down and pick up right where I left off. I don't have to go ask the paper to repeat itself, you know? So reading isn't really the issue. I read the sports page every morning while I have my coffee, and I don't have any trouble following the articles or keeping track of the stats. \n\nAnd as far as other tasks go, especially at work, I'm actually pretty good at staying on top of things as long as I'm moving. Like I said, I work production, so I'm on my feet all day. I'm checking the presses, I'm moving pallets, I'm clearing jams. I can stay focused on all that physical stuff for hours. It's actually when things slow down or when I have to stand in one spot and just listen to somebody that I start having problems. But if I'm actively doing something with my hands, or if I'm reading something at my own pace where I can control how fast the information is coming at me, I'm generally okay. It's just the talking. The talking is what gets me every single time.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, do other sights or sounds around you easily pull your attention away?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's not like a squirrel runs by the window and I completely lose my train of thought, you know? It's not the stuff going on around me that's the problem. Like at the plant, there's always a million things happening—forklifts beeping, guys shouting over the machines, sparks flying from the welding stations—and none of that really pulls my attention away when someone is talking to me. I can tune all that out just fine. \n\nIt's more like... the distraction comes from inside my own head, not from the outside world. If my supervisor is telling me about a change in the shift schedule, I'm not looking over his shoulder at a forklift. I'm looking right at him. But my brain just decides, right in that exact moment, that it's the perfect time to wonder if I left the garage door open, or to try and remember the name of that actor from that movie we watched last night, or to start thinking about what I'm going to make for dinner. It's like my own thoughts are just shouting over the person who's standing right in front of me. \n\nSo it's not the environment. The environment could be completely silent, like if I'm sitting in the living room with my wife and the TV is off and the dog is asleep, and she starts talking to me about her day. There are zero distractions in the room. Nothing to look at, nothing to listen to except her. But even then, my brain will just start spinning its own wheels, and before I know it, she's looking at me waiting for an answer and I have absolutely no idea what she just said for the last two minutes. So, yeah, it's not the outside sights and sounds at all. It's just my own head getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: In your day-to-day life now, do you often lose or misplace things you need, like keys, your phone, tools, or paperwork?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm actually pretty organized when it comes to my stuff. It's funny, because you'd think with how my brain bounces around, I'd be losing things left and right, but I actually have a real system down. \n\nLike with my keys and my wallet, the second I walk in the door at home, they go right into this little wooden bowl we keep on the table in the entryway. My wife bought it at a craft fair a few years ago—it's got this nice cherry finish—and I just trained myself to drop everything in there immediately. If I don't put it in the bowl, I know I'll put it down on the kitchen counter, or the coffee table, or leave it in my jacket pocket, and then I'd be running around like a madman the next morning. But because of the bowl, I always know exactly where they are. Same thing with my phone. It's either in my front right pocket, or it's on the charger by the bed. There's no in-between. \n\nAnd at work, man, you can't be losing tools on the production floor. That's a huge safety hazard, plus it just slows everything down. I've got my tool belt set up exactly the same way every single day. Wrench goes here, gauge goes there. If I take something out to adjust a machine, it goes right back into its specific pouch the second I'm done. I don't set it down on the line, I don't hand it to the guy next to me. It goes back in the belt. I've been doing this job for almost twenty years, and I can probably count on one hand the number of times I've actually misplaced a tool. \n\nEven with paperwork, like the timecards or the shift reports, I just have a clipboard. I clip it in, I fill it out, I hand it to the foreman. I don't leave it sitting around to get buried under other stuff. So yeah, losing things really isn't an issue for me at all. I've just built these habits over the years to make sure everything has a place, and I stick to it pretty religiously. If I didn't, maybe it would be a problem, but the way I do things now, I keep track of my stuff just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things in your daily life, like errands, chores, or returning calls?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty good about getting things done. It's kind of like what I was saying about my tools and my wallet—I just have my routines, you know? Like with chores around the house, my wife and I have this system we've been using since we bought the place back in '08. It's a modest house, three beds, one bath, but keeping it up takes work. So Saturday mornings, I wake up, I make the coffee—always a dark roast, none of that flavored stuff—and while it's brewing, I take the trash out. Then I mow the lawn if it's summer, or shovel the walk if it's winter. It's just muscle memory at this point. I don't even have to think about it, I just do it. \n\nAnd errands, same deal. If we need groceries or something from the hardware store, I usually just hit it on my way home from the plant. I get off at 3:30, so I beat the rush hour traffic, and I just swing by the store. My wife will text me a list, and I just go right down the aisles. I don't really forget to do it because it's just part of the drive home. If I go straight home and sit down, yeah, it's harder to get back up and go out again, but as long as I'm already in the truck and moving, I knock it out. \n\nReturning calls... I mean, I'm not much of a phone guy anyway. If someone calls me and leaves a voicemail, I'll listen to it, and usually I just call them right back. Or I'll text them. It's this big magnetic calendar with a picture of a golden retriever on it—looks just like the dog we had when I was a kid, actually. But yeah, I write it on there, and then I don't forget. Standing there checking boxes on a sheet or inspecting every little barcode, it just drives me completely up the wall. So I admit, I tend to just sort of fly through it. I’ll look at the unit, go check, check, check, and just send it on down the line so I can get to the next one. \n\nBut because I’m rushing through those quality checks like that, I end up missing these stupid little things. Like, I'll miss a wrong label being slapped on the side, or maybe a loose fitting on one of the pressure valves. Just last Tuesday—or maybe it was Wednesday? No, it was Tuesday, because they were serving that terrible meatloaf in the cafeteria—I sent down a whole batch where the warning stickers had the wrong serial number on them. Or sometimes the brass fittings won't be torqued down all the way. It’s not like the whole pump is going to explode or anything, but it’s technically not up to spec. \n\nAnd then my supervisor, Dave—good guy, I've known him since we were in our twenties, his kid actually plays travel baseball with my nephew—he has to come over and pull me aside. He actually takes me off the production floor and he\n\nDoctor: Have these attention problems caused serious trouble in more than one part of your life, like at both work and home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so like I was saying about Dave pulling me aside... to answer your question, yes. It's actually getting pretty serious. Like, really bad. Dave actually had to give me a formal warning. Two of them, actually, in the last six months. They bring you into this little glass office off the floor, and HR gets involved, and they print out this official form—it's always on this weird yellow paper, I don't know why they color-code it like that, but you have to sign it saying you understand the corrective action. And it really shook me up because, you know, Dave looked me right in the eye and said, \"Look, if you miss a loose fitting and one of these industrial pumps blows a seal during pressure testing, someone could get seriously hurt.\" And he's right. These things operate under massive pressure. If I cause a safety problem on the line because I can't just stand still and check the boxes properly... man, I worry I could actually lose my job. I really do. And I’ve been there forever. Starting over at 42 without a college degree? That keeps me up at night. \n\nAnd then, you know, it's not just the plant. It bleeds into home, too. I know I was bragging earlier about my Saturday morning routines with the coffee and the trash, but... well, the truth is, my wife and my kids, they get incredibly frustrated with me lately. Because a lot of the time, I don't actually help things run smoothly. I end up completely disrupting the routines. Like, I'll go to take that trash out, but on the way, I'll notice the hinge on the screen door is squeaking. So I drop the trash bag on the porch, go to the garage to find the WD-40, realize my workbench is a mess, start organizing my socket wrenches, and suddenly it's two hours later. The trash has been ripped into by a raccoon on the porch, the coffee I made burned to the bottom of the pot inside, and my wife is just... she's at her wit's end. \n\nShe tells me she just needs me to be a partner and help keep the house moving, but instead, I just throw a wrench in everything because I can't just do the one simple thing I set out to do. Even with the kids, when they're trying to get ready for school, I'm just buzzing around the kitchen, getting in their way, starting to make pancakes and then wandering off to check the weather on the TV. So, yeah... I guess when you put it all together, it really is causing serious trouble in pretty much every part of my life."
  }

Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. I have to go track him down on the factory floor and ask him to repeat the whole thing. He's getting really ticked off about it, looking at me like I'm an idiot or like I'm trying to mess with him. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah, I mean, as far as I know, my ears work perfectly fine. It's definitely not a volume thing, if that's what you mean. Like, I hear the actual sound coming out of their mouths loud and clear. It's not like they're mumbling or talking underwater or anything like that. \n\nTake my wife, for example—she speaks perfectly clear, enunciates everything, especially when she's getting frustrated with me. I hear every single syllable. I hear the tone of her voice, I know she's speaking English, I know she's standing right there. The physical sound is definitely making it into my ears. Even at the plant, you know, with all the machines going—the stamping presses are really loud, sure, it's a lot of heavy metal-on-metal crashing all day long—but when my supervisor gets right up in my face to talk, I can hear his voice over the background noise just fine. \n\nSo it's not my hearing. I don't need a hearing aid or anything like that. It’s more like... well, it’s like the words hit my eardrum, and they’re perfectly clear words, but the wire connecting my ear to my actual brain is just... unplugged or something. The sound goes in, it bounces around in my head for a split second, and then it just evaporates into thin air before my brain can actually translate it into an actual instruction or meaning. I'll even catch myself thinking, \"Wow, she's talking right now, I should listen,\" but the actual words just slide right off me. So, yeah, the hearing part is totally fine. It’s the keeping it in my head part that's completely shot.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, not really. I mean, it's not like a squirrel runs by the window and I completely lose my train of thought, you know? It's not the stuff going on around me that's the problem. Like at the plant, there's always a million things happening—forklifts beeping, guys shouting over the machines, sparks flying from the welding stations—and none of that really pulls my attention away when someone is talking to me. I can tune all that out just fine. \n\nIt's more like... the distraction comes from inside my own head, not from the outside world. If my supervisor is telling me about a change in the shift schedule, I'm not looking over his shoulder at a forklift. I'm looking right at him. But my brain just decides, right in that exact moment, that it's the perfect time to wonder if I left the garage door open, or to try and remember the name of that actor from that movie we watched last night, or to start thinking about what I'm going to make for dinner. It's like my own thoughts are just shouting over the person who's standing right in front of me. \n\nSo it's not the environment. The environment could be completely silent, like if I'm sitting in the living room with my wife and the TV is off and the dog is asleep, and she starts talking to me about her day. There are zero distractions in the room. Nothing to look at, nothing to listen to except her. But even then, my brain will just start spinning its own wheels, and before I know it, she's looking at me waiting for an answer and I have absolutely no idea what she just said for the last two minutes. So, yeah, it's not the outside sights and sounds at all. It's just my own head getting in the way.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. It's funny, because you'd think with how my brain bounces around, I'd be losing things left and right, but I actually have a real system down. \n\nLike with my keys and my wallet, the second I walk in the door at home, they go right into this little wooden bowl we keep on the table in the entryway. My wife bought it at a craft fair a few years ago—it's got this nice cherry finish—and I just trained myself to drop everything in there immediately. If I don't put it in the bowl, I know I'll put it down on the kitchen counter, or the coffee table, or leave it in my jacket pocket, and then I'd be running around like a madman the next morning. But because of the bowl, I always know exactly where they are. Same thing with my phone. It's either in my front right pocket, or it's on the charger by the bed. There's no in-between. \n\nAnd at work, man, you can't be losing tools on the production floor. That's a huge safety hazard, plus it just slows everything down. I've got my tool belt set up exactly the same way every single day. Wrench goes here, gauge goes there. If I take something out to adjust a machine, it goes right back into its specific pouch the second I'm done. I don't set it down on the line, I don't hand it to the guy next to me. It goes back in the belt. I've been doing this job for almost twenty years, and I can probably count on one hand the number of times I've actually misplaced a tool. \n\nEven with paperwork, like the timecards or the shift reports, I just have a clipboard. I clip it in, I fill it out, I hand it to the foreman. I don't leave it sitting around to get buried under other stuff. So yeah, losing things really isn't an issue for me at all. I've just built these habits over the years to make sure everything has a place, and I stick to it pretty religiously. If I didn't, maybe it would be a problem, but the way I do things now, I keep track of my stuff just fine.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. It's kind of like what I was saying about my tools and my wallet—I just have my routines, you know? Like with chores around the house, my wife and I have this system we've been using since we bought the place back in '08. It's a modest house, three beds, one bath, but keeping it up takes work. So Saturday mornings, I wake up, I make the coffee—always a dark roast, none of that flavored stuff—and while it's brewing, I take the trash out. Then I mow the lawn if it's summer, or shovel the walk if it's winter. It's just muscle memory at this point. I don't even have to think about it, I just do it. \n\nAnd errands, same deal. If we need groceries or something from the hardware store, I usually just hit it on my way home from the plant. I get off at 3:30, so I beat the rush hour traffic, and I just swing by the store. My wife will text me a list, and I just go right down the aisles. I don't really forget to do it because it's just part of the drive home. If I go straight home and sit down, yeah, it's harder to get back up and go out again, but as long as I'm already in the truck and moving, I knock it out. \n\nReturning calls... I mean, I'm not much of a phone guy anyway. If someone calls me and leaves a voicemail, I'll listen to it, and usually I just call them right back. Or I'll text them. Most of the guys at work just text anyway. If it's something official, like the dentist reminding me about an appointment, I just write it on the calendar we keep on the fridge. It's this big magnetic calendar with a picture of a golden retriever on it—looks just like the dog we had when I was a kid, actually. But yeah, I write it on there, and then I don't forget. So I wouldn't say I have a problem with forgetting to do things. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.
